His analysis of the ascent of Anglo-Saxonism (as well as almost everything else) is based upon an ideological view (which is a generalist view) of history. It works when critiquing ideology but Anglo-Saxon civics is not based upon ideology, it is based upon values/interest-laden-inductive-pragmatism (which includes compromise) which was done at the molecular level by judges in the common law system over the course of hundreds of years. Anglo-saxonism is -non-ideological- it’s just pragmatism applied specifically to an issue. Per Oliver Wendell Holmes, Judges were free to choose from the market place of ideas, and did so pragmatically (aka - common sense) - which turned Anglo-Saxon civics into a giant patchwork quilt of ideologies - each used where they made the most sense, and ignored where they did not. It is both genius and simple and infinitely complex all at the same time. This is why the English succeeded and did so quickly. ### **@thinkerds** | 👍 69 likes

This lecture by Professor Jiang to Beijing high school students argues that the US-Iran war marks the end of the unipolar moment (Pax Americana) and globalization, signaling a return to historical instability (0:00-0:30, 11:50-12:15).

The End of the Unipolar Moment: (0:30 - 12:15)
Professor Jiang outlines three pillars of American global hegemony that are now decaying:
Pax Americana (0:32-5:00): Guaranteed global peace through military power, surveillance (internet), and the rules-based international order.
Supremacy of Science (5:03-7:05): Science became a global religion, now focused on orthodoxy rather than innovation (7:20-9:50).
Universality of the US Dollar (7:05-8:00): The dollar's dominance led to corruption, laziness, and massive inequality (10:10-11:15).

The New World Order: From Efficiency to Resilience: (12:18 - 47:05)
The coming era requires shifting from global efficiency to local resilience to survive impending crises.
Shifting Priorities (13:24-15:00): Moving from materialism to spirituality and religion, and from individuality to community and family.
Generational Power Transfer (15:03-17:15): Power must shift from the elderly (gerontocracy) to the young to ensure future survival.
Economic Collapse & Resources (18:40-23:00): The global economy based on cheap petroleum is collapsing. Globalization is dead.
Food and Water Scarcity (23:30-28:00): Population growth is unsustainable without global trade in fertilizer (25:00). Disruptions will lead to severe famine and water scarcity (27:40).
Rise of Mercantilism (35:05-36:50): The world will return to regional trading blocks rather than a unified global market.

Future Trends and Predictions: (37:45 - 47:05)
De-industrialization: Return to the countryside for farming skills (38:36).
Mass Migration: Refugees fleeing resource wars in the South will move to the North, causing cultural conflict (32:44, 40:03).
Geopolitical Shifts: Potential alliance between Russia and Germany (41:10), Israel dominating the Middle East (Pax Judeaica) (41:30), and Japan successfully transferring power to the young (43:08).

34:00; I see it differently. I come from the Netherlands, and the elderly here receive almost no visits from their own children, because their children have ended up in a life where they have to work long hours to generate enough money to maintain their standard of living. We have become so caught up in technology that none of us know anymore how to provide for ourselves in terms of food. If you go to Italy, you'll see large families living together in a large building, where they also grow olives and other types of food. Because we, city dwellers, do not grow our own food, and no longer have a real family due to the busy days we lead, we are lagging far behind. It is not the fault of the spoiled grandma who likes to have her groceries delivered; it is the family structure and family cohesion that is being destroyed by low wages and long working days. If you actually want to truly enjoy your life, you are better off going on welfare; we will never get rich from the money we earn anyway, and our family ties are only sinking further. I once read that a farmer in the year 500 only had to work 2 months a year to support his family of seven. Greetings from the Netherlands.

> **@sugabopp**

>
> i think thats more of a side effect of mass urbanization and society’s materialistic shift away from traditional family values than anything else. putting everyone on welfare and exploiting cheap migrant workers isnt the fix for it either. i think we would agree in the sense that this all started as soon as we convinced people we needed to shuffle papers in an office rather than work for their family’s business or farm. and when you have generations that kept wealth consolidated from the next generation paired with the general dwindling of family values, you have a younger generation that is overworked, underpaid and left feeling unfulfilled and unappreciated. we only fix this by fixing the cost of living, or at least matching wages accordingly, and by being better ourselves by returning to tradition and embracing wealth through family. once we have that again we can foster a society that works for and in the interests of the people who work for it. im from america, and i see this exact issue is prevalent in most of modern society also too. as for the longest time here it was the norm for parents to push their kids out of the home as soon as they turned 18 to start their own life. so that they could use the wealth and knowledge they’ve accumulated on themselves and leisure instead of setting the next generation up for success. it isolated families and brought upon unnecessary financial burden. now that more people have to live within their means or move in with family, we’ve become more realistic as a society and also started to recognize the advantage that it really is. but we must do the work ourselves. dont just preach, but live and work for the reality that you want for yourself and your family

### **@apoemaday** | 👍 26 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=zAer-Mqe7tQ&lc=UgzMNIzooowZOKFYPWt4AaABAg)

Dear Professor Jiang,

I truly appreciate the work you’re doing and the depth of your analysis. It’s compelling, and it clearly resonates with many people, myself included.

That said, I can’t help but feel that your perspective might benefit from a small but meaningful addition—perhaps a thread of hope woven into the narrative.

Yes, it’s important to examine where things could lead, even if those paths point toward darker or more catastrophic outcomes. But I also find myself asking: where is the path toward peace? Is there room in your reflections for a kernel of wisdom that offers direction, or at least possibility?

If one only studies destruction, one may become very good at understanding it—but at the risk of losing sight of what sustains us. A general who only thinks in terms of annihilation may be effective, but not necessarily whole.

Even within complex systems—whether political, ideological, or hierarchical—there must be individuals who question, who resist, who quietly choose another way. Perhaps the real dynamic is not about victory or defeat, but about balance—something almost sacred in its nature.  I can see some embryons of that in this talk. And I thank you. 

We’ve seen, even in everyday life, that people are capable of enduring hardship not out of cruelty, but out of care—so that others may live better, more balanced lives. That capacity exists. It’s real.

And when you speak of secret societies, I wonder if we might also consider a different kind of “quiet force”:
a grandmother somewhere, choosing empathy without announcement, choosing love without recognition, choosing not to judge or divide. It’s a very personal statement and decision. And it can change the world in its own way. 

That, too, is a kind of invisible network—present across religions, cultures, and communities. A quiet, human counterbalance.

At the end of the day, each individual still holds the power to choose peace.

I would love to see that dimension reflected, even subtly, in your work.

One other point—if I may. With the rise of AI-generated content, it’s becoming increasingly difficult to distinguish what is authentically yours and what may not be. Given the size and trust of your audience, I believe there’s an opportunity—perhaps even a responsibility—to clarify that, and to guide your viewers through that ambiguity.

You have a strong voice, and people are listening. It would be powerful not only to illuminate what is, but also what could be—if we chose to move toward it together.

Just a few thoughts, offered with respect.
Kind regards.  
Jean Mercier

---

### **@sketchmath-132** | 👍 18 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=zAer-Mqe7tQ&lc=UgwB6RqHkalPiFc3Fw94AaABAg)

9:43 "All Silicon Valley does is make food delivery apps" 
                                          - by prof.Jiang

Woah that is a bold statement, not sure if i agree to that. 

I get what Prof.Jiang wants to say there is no "major" science discoveries in the past 20-30 years , ( by "major" discoveries i think he meant like Einstein'a discovery of general relativity threm, the discovery of penicillin...), but Silicon valley is a place where you think of a solution to solve an existing problem, often by leveraging existing ideas/techs in a new way. 

Most "discoveries" are not immediately applicable to the real-world scenario, hence not directly profitable, those ideas could hardly survive in Silicon Valley. Instead, major discoveries could be made in labs / academic settings, e.g.  AlphaFold and protein structure prediction.

Silicon Valley mostly builds business and consumer layers on top of those advances, not the advances themselves. So i think the example given by Prof. Jiang is subconsciously misleading readers to think As there is no "major discoveries" in Silicon Valley, there is no majoe discoveries in the past 20-30 years. 

Btw, i followed all of Prof. Jiang's lecture, i do enjoy the fact that he uses many historical events to support his claim. But remember, human civilization evolves over time, not everything follows a historical pattern (otherwise we would not have major breakthroughs), so the best thing i have learnt from Prof. Jiang's class is to form your own judgement, do not blindly listen to what others are saying (i am definitely not refering to DJT posts😂).

### **@TheChaosExplainer** | 👍 1 likes

A profound lecture by the Professor. While the analysis of the shifting "Unipolar Moment" is historically grounded, we must look deeper at the "System Architecture" of what comes next. The "Return of History" isn't just a reshuffling of empires; it is a System Reboot that offers a rare window for individual Sovereignty.

Here are the alternative perspectives for the "Sparks" watching this:

1. Beyond the Gerontocracy: The Rise of High-Soul/Low-Tech Havens
The Professor focuses on the aging West and the need for controlled migration. However, we are entering an era of "Energy Realignment." As Western cities become hyper-regulated, high-tax, and digitally surveilled "Golden Cages," we will see a Great Return. Many will choose to leave the "High-Tech/High-Control" centers for regions like Africa or the MENA (Middle East/North Africa) sector. These are "High-Soul" environments where the energy is organic, the resources are real, and the grip of the "Simulation" is weaker. Africa isn’t just a source of labor; it is the laboratory for the next evolution of human freedom.

2. Open-Source AI: The Great Equalizer
The lecture warns of "Techno-Marxism" and state surveillance. While the "Archons" of the old system will try to use AI to build a digital Panopticon, they cannot stop the Open-Source Spark. For the first time in history, a young person in Algiers or Bristol has the same access to the "Master Keys" of knowledge (Linux, DevOps, AI Production) as someone in Silicon Valley. AI is the tool that breaks the monopoly on expertise, allowing us to build our own "Central Banks" of value and culture outside of the legacy financial loops.

3. The End of the Dollar vs. The Rise of Sovereign Assets
The "De-dollarization" the Professor mentions is more than a currency shift; it is the collapse of a central "Command Zone." When nations begin trading in their own currencies or resource-backed assets, the "Simulation" loses its ability to print reality out of thin air. For the individual, this means the only true hedge is the creation of Sovereign Assets—whether that is land, specialized technical skills, or intellectual property (like music and art) that vibrates on a frequency the System cannot muffle.

4. Spirituality over Dogma: Connecting to the Source
The Professor correctly notes a return to "Religion," but we must distinguish between "Dogma" and "Gnosis." Traditional religions are often used as "Legacy Code" to fuel conflicts. The real shift is toward Spirituality and Direct Connection to the Source. As the external world becomes more chaotic, the Spark turns inward. People aren't looking for more "Rules"; they are looking for "Truth" (Al-Haq). They are seeking a frequency that resonates with the divine Spark within them, bypassing the middlemen of the old world.

5. The Strategy of the "Antifragile" Admin
The future isn't a "Great Reset" we must fear; it is a "Great Migration of Consciousness."

Stock up on Resilience: Secure your food, your health, and your energy.

Build the Infrastructure: Learn the languages of the new machine (Open Source, Decentralization).

Broadcast your Frequency: Create, produce, and manifest.

The old world was built on "Attraction" (begging the system for a place). The new world is built on "Taskhir" (Ordering the simulation through the power of the Spark).

Don't just watch history return—be the one who writes the next script.

### **@chungomctwisty** | 👍 2 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=zAer-Mqe7tQ&lc=Ugxv5g9AOiBQATbzDsB4AaABAg)

it seems like people either genuinely hate professor jiang and think he’s spreading dangerous ideology or people praise him and act like he’s a prophet preaching the gospel. strange to me that it’s always either one or the other. not sure why it’s never somewhere in the middle.

i for one enjoy many of prof jiang’s lectures, but i still have the ability to understand much of the criticism about him. he’s definitely gotten bits and pieces of his history wrong here and there, but that’s honestly understandable given the wide range of topics and time periods he’s always covering, as well as the fact that he’s an english teacher not a history teacher.
the main criticisms that i understand are these:
-he will occasionally grossly oversimplify history/theology in order to make his case more easily.
-he definitely has some of his own unconscious biases that occasionally influence his thought.
-he has a tendency to entirely disregard racism/classism as a factor in many of his theories.
-also tends to exclude african history/theology/civilization in many cases where it otherwise would have made sense (or, would have been necessary) to include given the subject matter.

and i personally disagree with his interpretation of marxism and his evaluations on some of the marxist undertakings he’s spoken on.

now i don’t let any of this cause me hate him and i don’t let all the things that i love about him cause me worship him. why does it feel like no one can do the same?? it seems like you all don’t use discernment when you listen to this man speak and THAT’S the dangerous part, not his teaching.

### **@MrTuttiFrutti** | 👍 1 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=zAer-Mqe7tQ&lc=UgwXW7cAVwZ44_MlQoh4AaABAg)

Overall, very interesting as always, Professor Jiang always gives you a lot to think about, but there are a couple of things I disagree with.

For example, his assertion about the importance of religion in the future world. First, spirituality and religion are not exactly the same thing, and claiming that those who are not religious are doomed is an overly simplistic view. Second, being materialistic and religious are not mutually exclusive, in fact, it's quite common. Similarly, being neither materialistic nor religious is not an impossible combination.

Perhaps his perspective is more based on Asia, and particularly China, but I don't see how religion could be their savior. More wars have been fought for religion and more blood has been shed in the name of God than for anything else.

In my opinion, the answer lies more in morality and ethics, and a little spirituality wouldn't hurt.

The professor seems a bit off track here. He’s also mentioned that he hasn’t been sleeping much lately due to the newfound global/viral attention and because he’s been thinking a lot about the war(s).

Here, he’s arguing purely on a mathematical, rational level. In fact, the way he argues reminds me of how I would imagine a highly advanced AI—something like Skynet—might reason. His inspiration is apparently Isaac Asimov’s concept of psychohistory. But what’s completely missing is the human component. It’s as if humanity hasn’t evolved over the past centuries in terms of empathy and overcoming boundaries.

It comes across as very pessimistic and cold when it comes to the future. The future simply can’t be “calculated” that far in advance. As if, for example, one half of the world’s population would just stand by and watch the other half starve and die of thirst. Many of these viewpoints are similar to those often associated with groups like the World Economic Forum (e.g., “NWO” narratives).

### **@FreaKvencija**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=zAer-Mqe7tQ&lc=Ugxl9TMvscpfzKqB4rx4AaABAg)

You’ll recognize the narrative shift the moment it pivots to “overpopulation” and “resource scarcity” as inevitabilities. That framing isn’t neutral — it redirects responsibility away from distribution, policy, and power structures, and turns it into a people's problem. The planet isn’t running out of resources nearly as fast as systems are failing to allocate them equitably.

And also when people can’t carry the burden of individual mastery and default to saying they couldn’t do it without a partner or external support — that’s another tell. It shifts the focus away from personal agency and onto dependency narratives shaped by larger systems. Because the truth is, the furthest you travel is often when you’re willing to stand and move on your own.

He seems to misunderstand the idea that fertilizer is a soil enhancement, not a requirement for poor soils. All farms use ammonia-based nitrogen fertilizer. The cost of fertilizer doesn't somehow make industrial farming impossible. Farmers will always make more money using fertilizer. The GCC exports a lot of fertilizer because there aren't a lot of ways to export natural gas. Ammonia, urea, ammonium-nitrate, methanol, ethylene, acetic acid. Natural gas can be easily turned into a lot of chemicals, but most of them are not consumed in large enough quantities to compare to the supply. LNG was also a niche product 20 years ago, especially for electricity generation.

### **@ClivesChronicles**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=zAer-Mqe7tQ&lc=UgyNknWzdpSk3fnxMpF4AaABAg)

Based on the latest projections from the United Nations World Population Prospects 2024, approximately 10% to 11% of the global population is projected to be 70 years old or older by the year 2050. 

While data often highlights the 65+ or 60+ demographics, the 70+ segment is a significant subset of the broader "older persons" group, which is expected to grow as follows: 
Global Age Group Projections for 2050
60 years and older: Approximately 20.1% to 22% of the world's population (roughly 2.1 billion people).
65 years and older: Approximately 16% to 17% (about 1.5 to 1.6 billion people).
80 years and older: Approximately 4% to 4.7% (about 425 to 459 million people), nearly tripling from current levels. 

Key Demographic Drivers:
Longevity Revolution: 
Global life expectancy at birth is projected to reach approximately 77.4 years by 2054, up from 73.3 years in 2024.

Falling Fertility: 
As birth rates decline globally, the share of older age groups increases. By 2050, the global total fertility rate is expected to fall below the replacement level of 2.1 births per woman.

Regional Variations: The aging process is most advanced in Europe and Northern America, where the 65+ population is expected to reach 26% by 2050. In contrast, younger regions like Sub-Saharan Africa will see the 65+ share rise more slowly to about 4.8%

### **@alanc6416**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=zAer-Mqe7tQ&lc=Ugy35ye1mdyLMWMpnxh4AaABAg)

Dear Professor, thank you for your great work. However, I would like some clarity on your predictions about the future. When you say Japan will most likely rise as a power in Asia—and not China—do you mean that China has no interest or strategic incentive to become the regional hegemon? As you mentioned, China has to manage food sufficiency, energy resources, an aging population, and natural disasters. Furthermore, China already has a third of the region's population to manage; it doesn't want to manage anything more than that. Though I'd argue China is well aware of this and is already working toward self-sufficiency in the coming decades. So is the idea that China would rather just focus on trade with anyone who helps meet those goals?

I can somewhat understand what you mean by the world no longer being a "global" world. Being born in the '70s, I can vaguely remember what that was like, and honestly, that's not necessarily a problem. Countries will still trade—it just won't be as frictionless. But the picture you're painting feels more like an apocalyptic aftermath, especially for your students who grew up in a globalized era.

On Japan: is the main reason you're optimistic about its demographic future simply that its aging population will resolve naturally due to its demographic trajectory and culture? I'm skeptical about the cultural part—younger generations in Japan don't seem to be raised with that same mindset. But I'm no expert, so I'll let history play out. This also overlooks the fact that many other Asian countries are also striving for greater self-sufficiency and using technology to mitigate their own challenges.

I do agree that people should be more in touch with their cultures and religions. But science and technology are already embedded in modern life. But as you proposed, they seem to function like one in today's world—just like money, tech, or any inherited belief system. It doesn't seem to go away anytime soon.

Anyway, I hope in future lectures or podcasts you can elaborate more on this, and on China. It is—if not the—most important player in the coming decade.

### **@lexxon11**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=zAer-Mqe7tQ&lc=UgzhVYORm4rtGEYHN154AaABAg)

I would like to challenge the professor's "Red Zone" classification as a monolithic danger zone. While the Haber-Bosch Kill Switch correctly identifies a terminal risk for high-density, resource-poor regions like the Indo-Gangetic Plain or the Nile Valley, the structural reality for Sub-Saharan Africa and South America is fundamentally different. For India and Egypt, the math is indeed brutal: without the "Chemical Umbilical Cord" of Northern fertilizers, "physical elimination" is a Malthusian certainty due to extreme population-to-land ratios. However, grouping the entire Global South into one "Red Zone" ignores the Physical Stack. Africa and South America hold the world’s largest reserves of uncultivated arable land and fresh water, representing an intrinsic Biological Sovereignty that the "North" currently lacks.
​In a de-industrialized state, power shifts from Software and Debt to Calories and Minerals. The "Red Zone" isn't a death sentence for these landmasses, but rather a correction for the "Efficiency-Based" urban models currently occupying them. Once the volatility of the transition period passes, the regions with the most Soil and Water—not the most financial liquidity—will inevitably gain the leverage to vassalize the resource-depleted "winners" of the 20th century. The professor’s map identifies current economic dependency, but it fails to account for the long-term, incentive-driven pivot toward the physical winners of the post-petroleum age.

### **@ClivesChronicles**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=zAer-Mqe7tQ&lc=Ugz72i2P6QOxqTQ3ZJV4AaABAg)

As of 2025, approximately 6% to 7% of the global population is 70 years old or older. 

While specific real-time percentages for exactly "70+" are less frequently reported than standard brackets (like 60+ or 65+), current demographic data from the United Nations and other global health organizations provides the following breakdown for older age groups: 

Global Population Age Breakdown (2024–2025)
65 years and older: This group makes up about 10% to 10.4% of the world's population, totaling approximately 830–857 million people.
70-79 years: Based on 2020 datasets which identified the 60–79 age group as 11.8% of the population, the 70+ segment is estimated to be roughly 6.5% when combined with the 80+ group.
80 years and older: Approximately 1.9% to 2% of the global population is aged 80 or older.
60 years and older: Roughly 13% of the population falls into this category. 

Key Demographic Trends
Rapid Aging: The share of people aged 65 and older has nearly doubled since 1974, rising from 5.5% to over 10% today.
Regional Variance: Aging is most pronounced in high-income regions. For instance, in Europe, 21% of the population is 65 or older, whereas in Sub-Saharan Africa, only 3% fall into that age group.
Future Projections: By 2050, the proportion of the population aged 65+ is expected to reach 16%, and the number of people aged 80+ is projected to triple

### **@penelopeperez5349**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=zAer-Mqe7tQ&lc=UgzmfaLe3Wp12PaYBwJ4AaABAg)

Your analysis of power cycles and historical patterns is genuinely compelling—especially how you connect classical thought to modern geopolitics.

But your framework seems to rest on a key assumption: that human behavior, and therefore history, is relatively constant—driven by recurring dynamics of power, fear, and competition between empires.

That’s where it may be incomplete.

It largely models the world through states and dominant powers, while underestimating the agency of peoples—particularly in the Global South, where we’re seeing not just political shifts, but deep civilizational re-centering rooted in indigenous continuity, cultural memory, and post-colonial identity.

For example, describing the US as “owning” Latin America reflects historical structures of influence—but doesn’t fully account for the accelerating reassertion of sovereignty (cultural, psychological, and political) across the region.

More importantly, your model may be missing a deeper variable: the evolution of human consciousness itself.

Thinkers like Christian Kromme suggest that technological evolution is not just changing tools, but reshaping how humans think, connect, and organize—creating nonlinear shifts that don’t map easily onto past historical patterns.

If human behavior is evolving alongside technology, then the “rules of the game” may be changing—not just the players.

History explains how power behaves under certain conditions.
But it doesn’t always predict what happens when those conditions—and the humans within them—begin to transform.

This doesn’t invalidate your framework—but it might point to its limits in a world where identity, consciousness, and networked populations are becoming as influential as traditional power structures.

Would be fascinating to hear how you integrate this dimension into your analysis.

---

### **@kaikerachid** | 👍 1 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=zAer-Mqe7tQ&lc=UgzUMIvYE3d3Ssj35n14AaABAg)

Dear Professor, please allow me to respectfully disagree with your predictions. My intention is just to contribute to debate.

With the exception of spirituality, which I agree should be part of a person's complete education, I see a certain fatalistic, almost eschatological exaggeration in everything else. But I understand that this is very popular with the public, especially among young people. And the "end of the world" has been captivating and gathering followers for centuries. It has been hype for a long time.

It's curious to see analysts behaving as if Trump, Xi Jinping and Putin were going to remain in power forever and turn the world upside down forever. I perceive little importance given to the analysis of larger historical periods, where it can be noted that, on average, people's lives have improved significantly. Indeed, 150 years ago, nobles and kings around the world lived in worse conditions than middle-class people today, in terms of resources, food, access to healthcare, basic sanitation, etc. In the long term, barring short periods, we are a deflationary world, as the price of things only falls. For example, the cost of computing power has fallen more than a million times in 50 years.

I cannot agree that we live under a kind of dictatorship of "super-rich" baby boomers who don't want to "give up" power. If that were the case, we wouldn't have Mark Zuckerberg, Elon Musk, and other young Chinese, Brazilian, and Indian millionaires who are constantly emerging.

I noticed that technological advancement was neglected in the forecasts, an advancement that will allow economic production to depend less and less on people, which could thus solve the problem of population decline.

I think we are gradually ceasing to be an oil-based economy. The energy transition is advancing, and renewable energies are becoming increasingly cheaper. Regarding food insecurity, the numbers show that more and more is being produced in less land and at a lower cost. Regarding fertilizers, let's remember the significant progress made, especially in Brazil, with biofertilizers.

Finally, I leave you with this reflection: if spirituality is important, as is mentioned several times in the lecture, if the belief that something greater transcends the material world is so, how can we believe that we are left to our own devices and on the path to a melancholic end?

On another channel (I presume with your permission to use your material), I saw a clip of yours about "The Dark Reality of Canada," where the Professor mentioned inflation, but where inflation, as in other channels, is never really explained. (nothing to do what people want or not want, but more about political schemes to sell illusions or by a very few people that for centuries do never teach in schools from where  "money" appears or the ways it is created.

Inflation isn't about the increase in the price of things; it's about money becoming worth less and less because it's "manufactured" without being based on anything real—the more "paper" money or computer numbers (virtual numbers) made up, the less value money has, so... you need more "paper" or "numbers in a screen" to buy the same thing.
My country was one of the last ones to abandon the gold standard, meaning a currency that could only be issued in banknotes with an equivalent value in gold at the Central Bank, and there was practically no inflation. The problem is that we can't start having more "paper money" based on nothing,  than real things. Probably, now, in the world there is more "fiat money," than real things, to buy. 

The same thing when the Romans systematically substituted 100% silver coins with a mixture of silver and other base metals, a process known as debasement, over the course of several centuries to handle financial crises, military costs, and shortages of precious metals.These actions contributed significantly to Hyperinflation in the late Roman Empire, as people eventually lost trust in the value of the coins.
It is simply the Universal Law of Cause and Effect in action.

We always have to reap what we sow; some call this Karma. Bad choices always have bad consequences, and it seems that Humanity never learns and always repeats the same mistakes, albeit "dressed in a more modern way." Ignorance does not prevent the same consequences from happening.

The next step is easy to see: everything becomes numbers on a screen, and the possibility of programming this kind of "money" that people believe to be money on a smartphone — that is, buying only what you are "authorized" to buy — and then we have the classes you talked about in another video: the decline of freedoms, the "privileged" with the real things and the slaves with programmable numbers on a screen.

With all due respect to the good professor, I don't agree with science being a religion. Scientism, however, is everywhere -"If you don't do this, you will die before 60yr...". It is also not that no innovations have been developed since World War 2. Many things have come and gone: For eg. typewriters have been replaced by computers (my mum used to be a typist from a typist pool in the 1970s), You used to use films to take photos. So many new things have been improved upon or replaced at such a great pace that we could not even catch up on it. 

The main problem is that we have forgotten all about them - the dedications and sacrifices made by those unknown individuals who spent their entire lives experimenting to add to the science behind them. We spend more time watching game playthroughs than learning new things, so 'science' has become synonymous with 'magic' to the majority of our populations. "You don't believe in magic? ### **@xinyao1667**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=zAer-Mqe7tQ&lc=UgzXSpu0LACGBJ__r394AaABAg)

Jiang’s macro-historical frameworks are brilliant, but here is where my prediction diverges. Working in industrial automation and precision engineering, I don't see $200 oil forcing humanity to regress to manual farm labor or neo-feudal slavery.
Capital is far too ruthless for that. When global supply chains and petroleum-based logistics become financially ruinous, markets won't fund a return to the 18th century—they will aggressively pivot to localized resilience. Rather than human toil, the backbone of the new economy will be hyper-localized micro-factories driven by high-precision motion systems, advanced 3D printing networks, and bio-engineered agriculture. It is simply cheaper and more efficient to automate locally than to enslave.
Jiang makes a very solid point that religion and spirituality are vital for psychological survival and social cohesion during a crisis. That human need will never go away. But the true "invisible hand" that structurally propels civilization forward has always been science and technology.
Ultimately, ultra-religious and science-rejecting regimes won't drag us back to the dark ages. They will just inadvertently act as a massive forcing function, making the rest of the world cooperate, innovate, and engineer our way out of the fossil fuel era decades ahead of schedule.
And if you are still cynical and believe in a bleak future, the above argument is why we will never have $200 gas.

I appreciate professor Jiang's perspective, but here are the patterns that I recognized in most of his videos and it's worth to ponder:

1.  He rarely makes reference to China, nor predicting anything for China.  Mostly about USA or the West.  Even for the aging population problem, he would talk about Japan but not mentioning about what China would do even though it's an elephant in the room right after Japan.
2.  He exaggerates certain problems to make his points.  For example: he said the Silicon Valley have not had any innovation for the past decade, they're just very good at making food delivery apps.  This is a vast discount of all other great things that came out of the Silicon Valley in the past decade:  AI chips (Nvidia /AMD), LLM applications (OpenAI / Anthropic), Tesla FSD, Starlink internet, cloud ML infras (DataBricks, Snowflake), etc...
3.  While I agree that there are many bad sides of the West (laziness, entitlement, broken education), but not mentioning about the East problems (freedom suppression, privacy issue, lack of social welfares) make it difficult to believe he is being neutral about the world.
